                     Biographical Sketch


The author was born in Metro Manila, Philippines. He attended the Ateneo de
Manila University, Philippines, and graduated with a Bachelor of Science degree
in Mathematics in 2007 and a Master of Science degree in Mathematics in 2010.
He began doctoral studies in Mathematics at the University of Rochester in 2011.
In 2013, he received a Master of Arts degree in Mathematics from the Univer-
sity of Rochester. He pursued his research in analytic number theory under the
supervision of Professor Steven M. Gonek.

   The following research publications were a result of work conducted during
doctoral study, and form part of this thesis:

   S. A. C. Baluyot, On the pair correlation conjecture and the alternative hy-
pothesis, J. Number Theory 169 (2016), 183-226.

   S. A. C. Baluyot, On the zeros of Riemann’s zeta-function on the critical line,
J. Number Theory 165 (2016), 203-269.

                                                                                  viii




                                  Abstract

This thesis has three parts. In the first part, we combine the mollifier method
with a zero detection method of Atkinson to prove in a new way that a positive
proportion of the nontrivial zeros of the Riemann zeta-function ζ(s) are on the
critical line. One of the main ingredients of the proof is an estimate for a mollified
fourth moment of ζ( 21 + it). We deduce this estimate from the twisted fourth
moment formula that has been recently developed by Hughes and Young.

   The second part of this thesis is concerned with bounding the number N (σ, T )
of zeros of ζ(s) that have real parts > σ and imaginary parts between 0 and T .
We prove a claim of Conrey that improves previous bounds for N (σ, T ) due to
Selberg and Jutila. We do this by constructing a new mollifier that allows us
to apply Conrey’s technique of using Kloosterman sum estimates to deduce an
asymptotic formula for the mollified second moment of ζ(σ + it) when σ is > 12
and close to 21 .

   In the third part of the thesis, we give a conditional proof of the equivalence
of certain asymptotic formulas for (a) averages over intervals for the 2-point form
factor F (α, T ) for the zeros of ζ(s), (b) the mean square of the logarithmic deriva-
tive of ζ(s), (c) a variance for the number of primes in short intervals, and (d)
the number of pairs of zeros of ζ(s) with small gaps. The main result is a gen-
eralization of the fusion of a theorem of Goldston and a theorem of Goldston,
Gonek, and Montgomery. We apply our result to deduce several consequences of
the Alternative Hypothesis.

1          Introduction and results



The function ζ(s) now known as the Riemann zeta-function is defined by
                                                ∞
                                                X 1
                                       ζ(s) =
                                                n=1
                                                      ns

when the complex variable s has real part > 1. In his epoch-making memoir of
1859, Riemann [55] outlined how one can study the prime numbers through this
function. We can see the connection between ζ(s) and the primes via the Euler
product formula
                            ∞               Y             −1
                            X 1                     1
                                        =        1− s            ,
                            n=1
                                  ns        p
                                                   p
where the product on the right-hand side is over the set of all primes p. In fact,
Riemann showed that ζ(s) has an analytic continuation to the entire complex
plane, and revealed the link between the zeros of ζ(s) and the primes. This
link can be seen more clearly from the explicit formula (see, for example, §17 of
Davenport [21])
    X              1            X xρ   ζ 0 (0)  1
          Λ(n) −     Λ(x) = x −      −         − log(1 − x−2 ),          x > 1.
    n≤x
                   2            ρ
                                  ρ    ζ(0)     2

Here, Λ(n) = log p if n > 1 and n is an integer power of a prime p, and Λ(n) = 0
otherwise. The sum on the right-hand side is over all the non-real zeros ρ of ζ(s).
The left-hand side of the explicit formula is an expression involving the primes,
                                                                                   2



while the right-hand side involves the zeros of ζ(s). This enables us to deduce
properties of the primes from properties of the zeros, and vice versa. Because of
this connection, a huge amount of work over the past century and a half has been
put towards locating the zeros.

   Today we know a good deal of information about the zeros of ζ(s). Rie-
mann [55] observed that ζ(s) has zeros at s = −2, −4, −6, . . . , called the trivial
zeros, and no other real zeros. He also observed that the non-real zeros, also called
the nontrivial zeros, are all contained in the critical strip

                             {s ∈ C : 0 ≤ Re(s) ≤ 1}.

In this strip, the zeros are symmetric about the line Re(s) = 12 . If N (T ) is
the number of nontrivial zeros, counting multiplicity, that have imaginary parts
between 0 and T , then (see, for example, §15 of Davenport [21])

                                    T       T
                         N (T ) =      log     + O(log T )                    (1.0.1)
                                    2π     2πe

as T → ∞. This formula was proved by von Mangoldt first in 1895 with a slightly
weaker bound for the error term, and then fully in 1905. Hadamard and de la
Vallée Poussin [64, §3.1] independently proved in 1896 that ζ(s) has no zeros with
real part equal to 1. Their discovery led them to the first proofs of the prime
number theorem, which states that if π(x) is the number of primes ≤ x, then

                                               x
                                    π(x) ∼                                    (1.0.2)
                                             log x

as x → ∞. This had been conjectured a century earlier by Gauss and Legendre,
and its proof highlights the striking power of ζ(s). The story, however, is far
from over. It turns out that the degree of precision of (1.0.2) depends on how
near the zeros are to the critical line Re(s) = 21 . In fact, Riemann conjectured
in his original memoir that all the nontrivial zeros lie on the critical line. This
statement is the famous Riemann Hypothesis, and to date is still an open problem.
                                                                                  3



The past decades have seen a lot of progress towards solving this and many other
problems about ζ(s), with many statements having been proved, and even more
having been conjectured. We will see indications of these throughout this thesis.

   In this thesis, we consider three problems about the distribution of zeros of
ζ(s). The first problem is that of showing that there are many zeros on the crit-
ical line. In Chapter 2, we devise a new method of proving that the critical line
contains a positive proportion of the nontrivial zeros of ζ(s). The second problem
is to bound the number of zeros that are off the critical line. Our current state
of knowledge is yet unable to rule out the existence of such zeros, but there are
bounds for the number of those inside certain regions of the critical strip. We
improve one such bound in Chapter 3. Finally, the third problem is to deter-
mine implications of the vertical distribution of the zeros along the critical line.
In Chapter 4, we consider hypothetical statements about this distribution, and
ascertain consequences for the prime numbers and other properties of ζ(s).

   Throughout this thesis, we use the following standard notations in analytic
number theory. The equation

                            f (T ) = g(T ) + O(h(T ))                        (1.0.3)

means that there exists some constant A, called the implied constant, such that

                             |f (T ) − g(T )| ≤ Ah(T )                       (1.0.4)

for all values of the variable T in a certain range. This range will be clear from
the context, and usually contains all T ≥ 0 or all large enough T . The expression

                            f (T ) = g(T ) + o(h(T ))

means that
                                  f (T ) − g(T )
                              lim                = 0.
                             T →∞     h(T )
We write
                                    f (T ) ∼ g(T )
                                                                                4



to mean
                            f (T ) = (1 + o(1))g(T ),

which is the same as limT →∞ f (T )/g(T ) = 1 when g(T ) is nonzero. As an alter-
native to (1.0.3), we may write

                             f (T ) − g(T )  h(T ).

We also write a  b to mean b  a. The expression

                                  f (T )  g(T )

means that both f (T )  g(T ) and f (T )  g(T ) hold. We sometimes use sub-
scripts to indicate the dependence of the estimates on certain parameters. For
example,
                             f (T ) − g(T ) b h(T )

means that the implied constant A in (1.0.4) is a function of a parameter b.



1.1       Zeros on the critical line

Recall that N (T ) is the number of zeros of ζ(s) in the critical strip that have
imaginary parts between 0 and T . Let N0 (T ) denote the number of these zeros
that are on the critical line Re(s) = 1/2. In Chapter 2 we give a new proof of the
following.

Theorem 1.1.1. The quantity

                                           N0 (T )
                               κ = lim inf
                                      T →∞ N (T )


is positive.

   We may interpret this theorem by saying that a positive proportion of the
nontrivial zeros of ζ(s) are on the critical line. This theorem was first proved
                                                                                   5



by Selberg [58] in 1942. The main new idea in Selberg’s work is to replace ζ(s)
by ζ(s)M (s) in a method of Hardy and Littlewood [34] for detecting zeros of
ζ(s). Here, M (s) is a certain function called a mollifier, and its purpose is to
diminish losses incurred in estimations. The idea is that inequalities such as the
Cauchy-Schwarz inequality become sharper when used with ζ(s)M (s) instead of
the function ζ(s) without a mollifier. Selberg observed that introducing a mollifier
in Hardy and Littlewood’s method improves their lower bound N0 (T )  T to
N0 (T )  T log T . This implies that κ > 0 because of the asymptotic formula
(1.0.1). The lower bound for κ that is implicit in Selberg’s proof is very small [64,
§10.9].

   Since Selberg’s introduction of the mollifier method, there has been a lot of
progress towards improving the lower bound for κ. All recent advances in the sub-
ject are based on a second approach to the problem, developed by Levinson [50].
The key component in Levinson’s technique is a method devised by Siegel [60] to
detect zeros of ζ(s). Siegel’s method alone leads only to N0 (T )  T , which is the
same thing that Hardy and Littlewood proved. Levinson combined this method
with the mollifier method, and the result is the second proof of Selberg’s theorem
with the stronger bound κ ≥ .3474. This approach has been improved further by
Conrey [15], who arrived at κ ≥ .4088 by using deep techniques involving Kloost-
erman sums. Conrey’s innovation is now the principal tool for pushing the lower
bound of κ higher, and more recent developments involve using stronger mollifiers
with Conrey’s results. One improvement is due to Bui, Conrey, and Young [12],
who showed that κ ≥ .4105. Another is due to Feng [27], who has claimed to have
proved κ ≥ .4128. However, Bui, Conrey, and Young [12] have observed that there
might be an error in his work. More recently, Robles, Roy, and Zaharescu [56]
have proved that κ ≥ .410725. A further step forward is due to Bui [11], who
used Conrey’s ideas together with a mollified third moment and a mollified fourth
moment of ζ(s) to prove κ ≥ .410918.
                                                                                    6



   In Chapter 2, we develop a third approach to the problem due to Atkinson [2].
The basic idea in Atkinson’s technique is that one can estimate the number of zeros
of a real-valued function f (t) by counting the number of times it changes sign.
This makes it similar to the method of Hardy and Littlewood, but Atkinson counts
the sign changes in a different way. The approach needs two main ingredients.
The first is an asymptotic formula for the mean-value
                          Z 2T
                               eia ζ( 21 + it0 )ζ( 21 − it) dt,                (1.1.1)
                               T

where a is a fixed real number and t0 is defined in a way that makes the integrand
real-valued for all t. The second ingredient is an estimate for the fourth moment
                                Z 2T
                                     |ζ( 12 + it)|4 dt.                     (1.1.2)
                                     T

Atkinson proves an asymptotic formula for (1.1.1) himself in [2], while he applies
the theorem of Ingham [42] to estimate the fourth moment. He uses these two
components together with the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to show that the in-
tegrand in (1.1.1) is often negative. This allowed him to estimate the number
of sign changes of the Hardy Z-function and deduce the lower bound N0 (T ) 
T (log T )−1 . We give a more detailed description of Atkinson’s method in Sec-
tion 2.1.

   The main idea in our proof of Theorem 1.1.1 is to combine the mollifier method
with Atkinson’s zero detection technique. We do this by inserting a mollifier
into each of the mean-values (1.1.1) and (1.1.2). Using the resulting expressions
in Atkinson’s argument makes the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality sharper and thus
reduces losses that occur from its use. The end result is an improvement of
Atkinson’s lower bound for N0 (T ) by two factors of log T . To reach this conclusion,
we need to evaluate both of the mollified mean-values. The first main result of
Chapter 2 is an asymptotic formula for a smoothed version of (1.1.1) with a
mollifier. We prove this using a method developed by Young [66] for estimating
                                                                                            7



a smoothed second moment of ζ(s) times a mollifier. The result is Theorem 1.1.2
below.

   The second main result of Chapter 2 is an asymptotic formula for a mollified
fourth moment of ζ(s). This is considerably more difficult to evaluate than the
second moment. The mollified fourth moment, or more generally the “twisted”
fourth moment, has received considerable attention in the past few decades. One
thing that makes the subject difficult is that any progress in it seems to depend
on the theory of Kloosterman sums. Iwaniec [46] was the first to give a nontrivial
upper bound for the twisted fourth moment
                          Z T                                      2
                                                 X
                                |ζ( 21 + it)|4         an n   it
                                                                       dt
                           0                     n≤N

                                                              it
                                            P
with an arbitrary Dirichlet polynomial           n≤N an n          . His bound was improved by
Deshouillers and Iwaniec [22; 23], and later by Watt [65]. Their bounds hold when
N is small compared to T , and are of size T 1+ε . The first asymptotic formula for
a twisted fourth moment was proved by Jose Gaggero Jara [28] in a Ph.D. thesis.
The formula turns out to be quite complicated, and Gaggero did not use it to study
the case when the Dirichlet polynomial is a mollifier. More recently, Hughes and
Young [40] have found a different way of proving an asymptotic formula. Their
method is based on the delta method of Duke, Friedlander, and Iwaniec [24],
which uses Weil’s bound for Kloosterman sums. Still another way of evaluating
the twisted fourth moment has been developed by Motohashi [53]. His method
uses the spectral theory of Kloosterman sums, and allows for a closer study of the
error terms in the asymptotic formula.

   Among the results we have described, the one we use to prove our formula
for the mollified fourth moment is the theorem of Hughes and Young [40]. Their
result reveals a permutational structure that is present in the main terms of the
asymptotic formula for the twisted fourth moment. This allows us to use a lemma
developed by Conrey, Farmer, Keating, Rubinstein, and Snaith [17] precisely for
                                                                                     8



evaluating functions with this structure. This lemma is Lemma 2.5.1 in [17], and
the special case we use is Lemma 2.5.1 below. To the author’s knowledge, the
mollified fourth moment that we consider in this thesis has not been evaluated
before. However, Conrey and Snaith [19] have formulated specific conjectures for
it by using the Ratios Conjectures. Their hypotheses also apply to the fourth
moment with a mollifier that is different from the one in this thesis. Moreover,
Bui [11] has proved a formula for this other fourth moment. To do this, he also
used a form of the theorem of Hughes and Young. Our result confirms a special
case of one of the two conjectures of Conrey and Snaith, while Bui’s verifies a
special case of the other conjecture. Our asymptotic formula for the mollified
fourth moment is Theorem 1.1.3 below.

   A few remarks about Theorem 1.1.3 are in order. First, note that we have
left C(a, θ) unspecified. In principle, we can compute the exact value of C(a, θ).
However, doing so by hand turns out to be a formidable task, and so we resort
to numerical calculations via computer software. We calculate C(a, θ) by writing
it in terms of the polynomial R(t; y, ρ) in (2.15.2) below. Hence we only need to
find the coefficients of R(t; y, ρ), and we can use any computer software that can
compute coefficients of polynomials. The resulting value we obtain for C(a, θ) is in
Section 2.19. The equation (2.15.2) results from simplifying certain path integrals
by writing their integrands as power series. Alternatively, we can evaluate these
path integrals by using the method of Conrey and Snaith [19, §6] of using matrix
determinants. This will lessen the amount of work needed to compute C(a, θ).
However, it does not seem to do so to the extent that we are able to calculate by
hand. Thus we adhere to our approach of using power series because it seems to
be the most straightforward.
                                                                                     11



   Aside from the main term C(a, θ), one may also take notice of the form (1.1.4)
of the mollifier M (t). In their proofs of Theorem 1.1.1, both Selberg [58] and
Levinson [50] use the “weight” factor
                                          log(y/n)
                                   $ =
                                            log y
in their mollifiers. On the other hand, we use the weight $2 , as one can see in
the definition (1.1.4) of M (t). Conrey [15] uses more general weights of the form
P ($), where P (x) is a polynomial with P (0) = 0. This weight is also used in the
above mentioned works of Bui [11], Bui, Conrey, and Young [12], and Feng [27].
We use the weight $2 so that we can deduce a useful formula, namely (1.1.8), for
the mollified fourth moment in a way that is not too cumbersome. At one extreme,
using the simpler weight $ leads to a different asymptotic formula that has a main
term of size W log T . Such a result cannot be applied to prove Theorem 1.1.1,
because our proof needs a main term of size W . At the opposite extreme, working
with a general weight P ($) makes the computations more complicated. Since our
goal is merely to show that κ > 0, we do not need the most general version of
Theorem 1.1.3, and we can focus on the simplest case with weight $2 . Our method
also works for the general case with weight P ($), where P (x) is a polynomial
satisfying P (0) = P 0 (0) = 0. It is left to the reader to make the slight changes
necessary to prove the general case.

   Theorem 1.1.3 does not overlap with the result of Bui [11]. Indeed, Bui uses a
different form of a mollifier, namely
                                  X µ2 (n)                 
                                                   log(y/n)
                      M2 (t) =          1/2+it
                                               P              ,
                                  n≤y
                                      n              log y

where µ2 (n) is the nth coefficient of the Dirichlet series for ζ −2 (s). To mollify the
fourth moment of ζ(s) with this mollifier is to multiply |ζ(s)|4 by |M2 (t)|2 in the
integral. In our case, we multiply |ζ(s)|4 by |M (t)|4 .

   Bettin, Bui, Li, and Radziwill [6] have recently improved the result of Hughes
and Young by increasing the range of h and k on which an asymptotic formula
                                                                                    12



for the twisted fourth moment holds. The theorem of Hughes and Young is valid
for hk ≤ T 2/11−ε . Bettin et al. have proved an asymptotic formula that is true
for h ≤ T 1/4−ε and k ≤ T 1/4−ε . Moreover, they show that the formula still holds
even when one averages over such h and k. Using their result in our proof of
Theorem 1.1.3, we would be able to deduce a version of Theorem 1.1.3 that is
true for θ in the extended range 0 < θ < 1/8, at least for certain forms of w(t).

   Having made our remarks about Theorem 1.1.3, we now return to the main
problem of this section. The lower bound for κ that we arrive at is very small; we
only reach
                                  κ > 0.0001049.

Moreover, we are only able to increase this by a small amount when we assume the
(still unproven) assertion that it is possible to take any positive θ in the hypotheses
of Theorems 1.1.2 and 1.1.3. Indeed, the largest lower bound for κ that we obtain
through numerical computations is ≈ 0.0086729 when θ ≈ 0.9967280. This seems
to indicate that Atkinson’s method of detecting zeros has an inherent weakness
that makes it difficult for us to apply it effectively. We deduced these estimates
by using the weight $2 . It is possible that these bounds can be improved by
employing a more general weight P ($) and choosing an optimal polynomial P (x).
However, we do not expect any improvements to be significant.

   One possible way of improving this approach is to modify it so that the lower
bound for κ tends to 1 as θ → ∞. This phenomenon was observed to hold for
Levinson’s method by Farmer [25]. As we have found in the above paragraph,
this does not hold for the current version of our approach. Sandro Bettin has
suggested to the author that perhaps this might not be the case if one instead
uses the mollifier
                                    X λ(n)  log(y/n) 2
                       M1/2 (t) =                             ,                 (1.1.9)
                                    n≤y
                                          n1/2+it   log y
                                                                                         13



where λ(n) is the nth coefficient of the Dirichlet series for ζ −1/2 (s). His suggestion
is to use the factor |M1/2 (t)M1/2 (t0 )|2 instead of |M (t)|2 on the left-hand side of
(1.1.6). Applying Cauchy-Schwarz as in (2.1.1) below would then result to in-
equalities involving |Z(t)|4 |M1/2 (t)|8 and |Z(t0 )|4 |M1/2 (t0 )|8 instead of |Z(t)M (t)|4
and |Z(t0 )M (t)|4 . The idea is that M (t) is an effective mollifier for Z(t), but a
poor one for Z(t0 ). Hence, using the mollifier (1.1.9) instead of (1.1.4) would elim-
inate losses caused by the incompatibility of M (t) and Z(t0 ). However, evaluating
the mollified mean-values of ζ(s) in this case is much more complicated. Thus this
is left for future work. We do not make any claims that this suggestion will lead
to any desirable results. In any case, it would be interesting to see how Atkinson’s
method can be further improved in this or in other ways.

   This section and Chapter 2, with slight changes, have been published in [5]
(http://dx.doi.org/10.1016/j.jnt.2016.01.026).



1.2      Zero-density estimates

Let N (σ, T ) denote the number of zeros β + iγ of ζ(s) that satisfy β > σ and
0 < γ ≤ T . Upper bounds for N (σ, T ) have a large number of applications to
problems in number theory. Because of this, a great deal of effort has been put
toward the estimation of N (σ, T ). Bounds for N (σ, T ) are usually called zero-
density estimates. The earliest zero-density estimate is due to Bohr and Landau,
who showed in 1914 that [64, §9.15]

                                     N (σ, T )  T

as T → ∞ for fixed σ > 21 . This was later improved by Carlson, who proved [64,
§9.17]
                                N (σ, T )  T 4σ(1−σ)+ε
                                                                                    14



as T → ∞ for fixed σ > 21 . Hoheisel [38] used a refinement of Carlson’s theorem
together with a zero-free region due to Littlewood to show that there exists an
absolute constant ϑ < 1 such that

                                  pn+1 − pn  pϑn

for all n, where p1 < p2 < · · · are the prime numbers. The value of ϑ that Hoheisel
arrived at was ϑ = 32999
                   33000
                         , which is very close to 1. Later, Ingham [43] proved that
if ζ( 12 + it)  tb then

                           N (σ, T )  T 2(1+2b)(1−σ) (log T )5

uniformly for 21 ≤ σ ≤ 1. Ingham then used this to improve Hoheisel’s theorem
by showing that it holds true for
                                          1 + 4b
                                   ϑ =           + ε.
                                          2 + 4b
                                                      577
This increased the range of admissible values of ϑ to 925 < ϑ < 1, by the known
values for b then. Note that if the Lindelöf Hypothesis ζ( 21 + it)  tε is true, then
Ingham’s bound for N (σ, T ) implies

                                N (σ, T )  T 2(1−σ)+ε                          (1.2.1)

uniformly for 21 ≤ σ ≤ 1. In this case, Hoheisel’s theorem holds with ϑ = 21 + ε,
which is almost as strong as the bound

                              pn+1 − pn  pn1/2 log pn

that would hold if the Riemann Hypothesis is true [20]. This is only one of many
examples for which a strong upper bound for N (σ, T ) can give results of nearly
the same quality as those implied by the Riemann Hypothesis.

   The bound (1.2.1) is called the density hypothesis, and proving it is currently
an open problem. However, much progress has been made toward it. Ingham [44]
proved that
                                              3(1−σ)
                             N (σ, T )  T 2−σ (log T )5                        (1.2.2)
                                                                                 15



uniformly for 12 ≤ σ ≤ 1. Using a method due to Halász, Montgomery [51] showed
that
                                           2(1−σ)
                           N (σ, T )  T      σ     (log T )14

uniformly for 54 ≤ σ ≤ 1. The Halász-Montgomery method has been further
developed by Huxley [41], who proved
                                           3(1−σ)
                           N (σ, T )  T 3σ−1 (log T )44

uniformly for 43 ≤ σ ≤ 1. Note that the density hypothesis (1.2.1) for the range
5
6
    ≤ σ ≤ 1 follows from Huxley’s theorem. Building on these developments,
Jutila [48] has extended the range on which (1.2.1) is known to hold to 11
                                                                        14
                                                                           ≤ σ ≤ 1.
A considerable number of other bounds for various ranges have also been proved
by Heath-Brown [36] and Ivić [45, Chapter 11]. More recently, Bourgain [10] has
proved that (1.2.1) holds for σ > 25
                                  32
                                     , thus improving Jutila’s result.

    A large number of advances have been made toward proving the density hy-
pothesis (1.2.1) for σ > 12 in ranges that are far from 21 . However, progress for σ
close to 12 has been slower. One reason for this is that the Halász-Montgomery
method only seems to work well when σ is far from 12 . The standard approach
to bounding N (σ, T ) when σ is close to 12 is to apply Littlewood’s lemma (see,
for example, (9.9.1) of Titchmarsh [64]). Using this approach, Selberg [59] proved
that
                           N (σ, T )  T 1+θ(1−2σ) log T                     (1.2.3)

uniformly for 12 ≤ σ ≤ 1, with θ = 18 . This supersedes (1.2.2) when

                          1       1   48 log log T
                            ≤ σ ≤   +              .
                          2       2    13 log T

Bounds of the form (1.2.3) are useful for studying arg ζ( 21 + it). In fact, this
is what Selberg does in [59]. Later, Jutila [49] improved Selberg’s theorem by
showing that (1.2.3) holds for 0 < θ < 12 . Note that the density hypothesis (1.2.1)
is equivalent to the statement that (1.2.3) holds for all 0 < θ < 1.
                                                                                  16



   Selberg proves (1.2.3) by bounding a mollified second moment
                          Z 2T
                               |ζ(σ + it)M (σ + it)|2 dt                     (1.2.4)
                             T

for 21 < σ ≤ 1, where M (s) is a certain function called a mollifier, as introduced
in Section 1.1 above. Selberg used a mollifier of the form
                                            X λn
                                 M (s) =                 ,                   (1.2.5)
                                                    ns
                                            n≤T θ

for some specific coefficients λn . Mollified moments such as (1.2.4) have a wide
number of applications to the study of ζ(s) and other L-functions. In using a
mollifier such as (1.2.5), it is most often the case that one can deduce better
estimates by increasing the value of θ. For instance, the upper bound (1.2.3)
decreases as θ increases. However, the evaluation of the mollified moment becomes
more delicate as the value of θ increases. To address this issue, Conrey [15] has
devised a technique that gives nontrivial estimates for values of θ larger than
what previous methods can handle. The key point of his technique is to use
Kloosterman sum estimates to approximate the sums arising from the analysis of
the mollified moment. As we have seen in Section 1.1, his technique allowed him
to prove that more than 40% of the zeros of ζ(s) lie on the critical line [15].

   In Chapter 3, we apply Conrey’s technique to the estimation of N (σ, T ) and
improve the bound (1.2.3). Our main result is the following.

Theorem 1.2.1. If 0 < θ < 47 , then

                            N (σ, T ) θ T 1+θ(1−2σ) log T

uniformly for σ ≥ 21 and large T .

   As mentioned above, this bound has been proved by Selberg for θ = 18 and
by Jutila for 0 < θ < 12 . Theorem 1.2.1 is not entirely new; in fact, it has been
announced by Conrey [14] in 1989. However, as far as the author knows, a proof
has never been published.
                                                                                   17



   The main new contribution in our proof of Theorem 1.2.1 is the use of a new
mollifier M (s) in (1.2.4) that is different from Selberg’s, Jutila’s, or Conrey’s.
This mollifier is
                                      X µ(n)  W (y/n) 
                          M (s) =                        ,                     (1.2.6)
                                       1+ε
                                           ns   W (y)
                                     n≤y

where y = T θ and the function W (ξ) is defined by
                               Z
                             1               ζ(2σ + s)
       W (ξ) = W (ξ; σ) =           Γ(s + 1)           ζ(1 + s)ξ s ds          (1.2.7)
                            2πi (r)            ζ(2σ)

for σ > 21 , ξ > 0, and r > 0. The use of (1.2.6) gives us relative ease in evaluating
the main terms in our asymptotic formula for (1.2.4), and at the same time allows
us to use the full power of Conrey’s technique in estimating the error terms.
The function W (ξ) is reminiscent of the functions ωj (ξ) of Soundararajan [61].
By choosing the mollifier M (s) in (1.2.4) to be (1.2.6), we prove the following
asymptotic formula, from which Theorem 1.2.1 will follow by standard arguments.

Theorem 1.2.2. Let M (s) be defined by (1.2.6) with y = T θ . If 0 < θ < 74 , then
there exists an 0 > 0 such that

1.3      The Alternative Hypothesis

The results described in Sections 1.1 and 1.2 are unconditional. That is, their
truth does not rely on any unproved hypothesis. On the other hand, in this
section and Chapter 4, we assume that the Riemann Hypothesis (RH) is true.
                                                                                                 18



   Let 12 +iγ denote a nontrivial zero of ζ(s). In the early 1970’s Montgomery [52]
introduced a new method of studying the distribution of zeros of ζ(s). Assuming
RH, he defined the function
                                               −1
                                     T                  X                  0
           F (α) = F (α, T ) =          log T                      T iα(γ−γ ) w(γ − γ 0 ),
                                     2π               0<γ,γ 0 ≤T


where the sum is over all pairs γ, γ 0 of ordinates of zeros counted according to
multiplicity. Here α is real, T ≥ 2, and w(u) = 4/(4 + u2 ). He then observed that
F is a real valued even function of α, and proved that

                     F (α, T ) = (1 + o(1))T −2α log T + α + o(1)                            (1.3.1)

uniformly for 0 ≤ α ≤ 1 − ε for any fixed ε > 0. From this he deduced that if RH
is true, then at least two-thirds of the zeros of ζ(s) are simple. He also showed
that if 0 < γ1 ≤ γ2 ≤ γ3 · · · is a list of the ordinates of all the zeros above the real
line, counted according to their multiplicities, then the differences

                               γn+1 log γn+1 γn log γn
                                            −                                                (1.3.2)
                                    2π          2π

are less than 0.68 infinitely often. Later, Mueller and Heath-Brown noted that F
is nonnegative (see [13]), and Goldston and Montgomery [33, Lemma 8] showed
that (1.3.1) holds uniformly for 0 ≤ α ≤ 1.

   The usefulness of F (α) for deducing information on the distribution of the
zeros is limited by the fact that the asymptotic behavior of F (α) is known only
for |α| ≤ 1. To get around this difficulty, Montgomery [52] used a quantitative
form of the Hardy-Littlewood twin prime hypothesis to conjecture that for any
fixed M ≥ 1,
                                  F (α, T ) = 1 + o(1)                                       (1.3.3)

as T → ∞, uniformly for 1 ≤ α ≤ M . This led him to make the following
conjecture.
                                                                                                       19



Conjecture (The Pair Correlation Conjecture). For any fixed β > 0, we have

                                    −1                             Z β                    2
                def       T                     X                                   sin πu
       N (T, β) =            log T                           1   ∼         1−                     du
                          2π                                          0               πu
                                             0<γ,γ 0 ≤T
                                                      2πβ
                                           0<γ−γ 0 ≤ log T

as T → ∞.

   The integrand above is called the pair correlation function of the zeros. It
happens that the eigenvalues of large random Hermitian matrices (the Gaussian
Unitary Ensemble or GUE), which are used in the study of particle physics, have
exactly the same pair correlation function. Thus the pair correlation conjecture
provided a surprising connection between number theory and random matrix the-
ory, two fields that seemed unrelated at the time. Later, Odlyzko [54] found
strong numerical evidence supporting the pair correlation conjecture. Bolanz [9],
building on Montgomery’s ideas, proved that (1.3.3) holds for 1 ≤ M < 32 pro-
vided that a strong form of the Hardy-Littlewood twin prime conjecture is true.
He later (unpublished) extended the range to 1 ≤ M < 2 under an additional
assumption. Hejhal [37] proved results similar to Montgomery’s for the triple cor-
relation function of the zeros of ζ(s), and Rudnick and Sarnak [57] proved results
for the n-correlation function, providing even more theoretical evidence that the
zeros are distributed like the eigenvalues of matrices from the Gaussian Unitary
Ensemble. Bogomolny and Keating [7; 8] used a prime-twin type conjecture to
heuristically extend the range of the n-correlation result of Rudnick and Sarnak.

   In spite of the overwhelming numerical and theoretical evidence, the pair cor-
relation conjecture has yet to be proved. Thus, it is of interest to determine con-
sequences of other conjectures about the spacings of the zeros. One well known
alternative to the pair correlation conjecture is the Alternative Hypothesis. There
are various formulations of it, but they all essentially say that almost all the dif-
ferences (1.3.2) are close to half-integers. In a sense, the Alternative Hypothesis
is antithetical to the pair correlation conjecture in that the latter says the zeros
                                                                                  20



are randomly distributed, whereas the former says they are quite regular. Besides
supporting the pair correlation conjecture, a disproof of the Alternative Hypoth-
esis would be useful in showing that Landau-Siegel zeros do not exist. Indeed,
Conrey and Iwaniec [18] have obtained a relation between the spacings (1.3.2) and
the size of L(1, χ) for real primitive Dirichlet characters χ. A corollary of their
Theorem 1.2 is that if the number of ordinates γn ≤ T for which the spacings
(1.3.2) are less than 0.49, say, is  T (log T )4/5 (as T → ∞), then

                               L(1, χ)  (log q)−90 .

The implied constant in their result is effectively computable.

   Conrey [16] formulates the Alternative Hypothesis as the existence of a func-
tion h(T ) that goes to 0 as T → ∞ such that if γn ≥ T0 , then the difference
(1.3.2) is within h(T0 ) of a half-integer. A more precise formulation is implicit in
a lecture of Heath-Brown [35]. He observed that if L(1, χ)  q −1/4−ε , then there
is a sequence of points tn that are close to the zeros of ζ(s)L(s, χ) such that if tm
and tn are about the size of T , with T much larger than q, then there is an integer
k with
              (tm − tn ) log T = πk + O(|tm − tn |{log q + log2/3 T }).

We will base our formulation of the Alternative Hypothesis on this. We let

                                          γ
                                    γ
                                    e=      log γ
                                         2π

denote the normalized ordinate of a zero, so that the average spacing between
consecutive γ
            en ’s equals 1. Our version of the hypothesis is the following.

To do this, we look at known consequences of the pair correlation conjecture and
determine their corresponding forms under the Alternative Hypothesis.

                                                                                     26




2           Zeros of the Riemann
            zeta-function on the
            critical line

In this chapter, we prove the results described in Section 1.1. All instances of the
function w(t) in this chapter depend on T . Thus the number W defined by (1.1.5)
and other integrals involving w(t), particularly those in Theorems 1.1.2 and 1.1.3,
also depend on T . We follow standard practice in analytic number theory and use
ε to denote an arbitrarily small positive constant that is not necessarily the same
for each instance. We consider the variable T > 1 as a large parameter going to
infinity.



2.1         Atkinson’s method and our starting point

In this section, we describe the key idea in the method of Atkinson [2] for deducing
a lower bound for the number of zeros of ζ(s) on the critical line. We present it in
a form that enables us to use Theorems 1.1.2 and 1.1.3 to prove Theorem 1.1.1.

    Since |Z(t)| = |ζ( 21 + it)|, each real zero of Z(t) corresponds to a unique zero of
ζ(s) on the critical line. Thus we can prove Theorem 1.1.1 by showing that Z(t)
has many zeros as a function of the real variable t. To do this, we show that the
                                                                                        27



sign of Z(t) changes often. We define S to be the set of t ∈ [T /2, 4T ] for which
Z(t)Z(t0 ) < 0. Atkinson’s approach is to prove a lower bound for the measure
of S. Using such an estimate, we can deduce a lower bound for the number of
critical zeros of ζ(s) by an elementary argument (see Section 2.18).

   To find a lower bound for the measure of S, let w(t) be a smooth nonnegative
function supported on [T /2, 4T ]. Apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality twice (or
Hölder’s inequality once) to write
 Z                                2
                   0       2
         |Z(t)Z(t )||M (t)| w(t) dt
     S
           Z ∞                            1/2 Z ∞                           1/2 (2.1.1)
  ≤ N              |Z(t)|4 |M (t)|4 w(t) dt          |Z(t0 )|4 |M (t)|4 w(t) dt    ,
              −∞                                  −∞

where                                    Z ∞
                                 N =           1S (t)w(t) dt
                                          −∞

and 1S (t) is the characteristic function of S. We can estimate the measure of S
from the size of N (see Section 2.18), and so it suffices to bound N from below.
We do this by dividing both sides of (2.1.1) by the two factors on its right-hand
side that are different from N . Hence, to prove a lower bound for N , we need
a lower bound for the mean-value on the left-hand side of (2.1.1) and an upper
bound for each of the fourth power moments on the right-hand side.

   We estimate the left-hand side of (2.1.1) as follows. Since Z(t)Z(t0 ) < 0 for
t ∈ S, it is true that
                                |Z(t)Z(t0 )| = −Z(t)Z(t0 )

for such t. Moreover, if t ∈ [T /2, 4T ] is not in S, then Z(t)Z(t0 ) ≥ 0. It follows
from these observations that
     Z                                  Z
         |Z(t)Z(t )||M (t)| w(t) dt = − Z(t)Z(t0 )|M (t)|2 w(t) dt
                 0         2
       S
                                        ZS∞                                        (2.1.2)
                                    ≥ −     Z(t)Z(t0 )|M (t)|2 w(t) dt.
                                                −∞
                                                                                  28



Thus we can use Theorem 1.1.2 to bound the left-hand side of (2.1.1) from below.
For the lower bound to be nontrivial, we will need to choose a value of a that
makes the right-hand side of (1.1.6) negative.

   We can use Theorem 1.1.3 to deduce upper bounds for the fourth power mo-
ments on the right-hand side of (2.1.1). The special case of Theorem 1.1.3 with
a = 0 immediately gives a formula for the fourth moment that does not involve t0 .
For the other one, we approximate it by the left-hand side of (1.1.8). We fill-in
the details and finish this argument in Section 2.18.

   The structure of the rest of the chapter is as follows. In Section 2.2, we state
basic lemmas and develop the machinery we need in our proof of Theorem 1.1.2.
In Section 2.3, we prove an asymptotic formula for a smoothed version of the
mean-value (1.1.1) with a factor (h/k)−it . We then use the formula to prove
Theorem 1.1.2 in Section 2.4. The starting point of our proof of Theorem 1.1.3
is in Section 2.5. There, we apply Theorem 1.1 of Hughes and Young [40] and
Lemma 2.5.1 of Conrey et al. [17] to write the mollified fourth moment as an
iterated integral. Sections 2.6 through 2.15 are devoted to simplifying this iterated
integral. We complete the proof of Theorem 1.1.3 in Section 2.15. In Section 2.16,
we construct a specific smooth function w(t) and prove Corollaries 1.1.1 and 1.1.2.
In Section 2.17, we apply Theorem 1.1.3 to evaluate the integral with Z(t0 ) on
the right-hand side of (2.1.1). We use our results from Sections 2.16 and 2.17 to
finish our proof of Theorem 1.1.1 in Section 2.18. Finally, we describe the results
of our numerical computations in Section 2.19.



2.2      Lemmas

Our first lemma is a generalization of the well-known Euler product formula.
Special cases of this have been implicitly used in various places in the literature.
We will need only the cases k = 2 and k = 4 in this thesis.

2.5      Application of the theorem of
         Hughes and Young

Our main tool in proving Theorem 1.1.3 is the formula for the twisted fourth
moment of ζ(s) that is due to Hughes and Young [40, Theorem 1.1]. In this
section, we apply their theorem to write the mollified fourth moment in terms of
an integral that can be evaluated using residue theory. We assume that w(t) is a
function satisfying the properties in the hypothesis of Theorem 1.1.3.

   We first express the formula of Hughes and Young in a form that is easier
to handle. To do this, we make a few definitions. Let Ξ be the set of permu-

    We next express the factor (ΨKρ )(α, β, γ, δ) in (2.5.6) as an integral. To do

Lemma 2.5.1. Let f be analytic in a neighborhood of s = 0 except for a simple
pole of residue 1 at s = 0. Suppose that F (α, β, γ, δ) is a function that is an-
alytic in each variable in a neighborhood of (0, 0, 0, 0) such that F (α, β, γ, δ) =
F (β, α, γ, δ) and F (α, β, γ, δ) = F (α, β, δ, γ). Let

          K(α, β, γ, δ) = F (α, β, γ, δ)f (α − γ)f (α − δ)f (β − γ)f (β − δ).
                                                                                              57



If α 6= γ, δ and β 6= γ, δ, then

   (ΨK)(α, β, γ, δ)
                        K(z1 , z2 , z3 , z4 ) 1≤j<`≤4 (zj − z` )2
                  IIII                       Q

where the path of integration is the same for all four integrals and encircles each
of the four points α, β, γ, δ exactly once in the positive direction. In particular,
ΨK is analytic at (0, 0, 0, 0).

   Now let ω 6= 0 and set α = β = ω and γ = δ = 2ω in (2.5.6). We apply
Lemma 2.5.1 to the right-hand side of the resulting equation, and then take the
limit of both sides as ω → 0. The result we arrive at is stated in the following
proposition.

Proposition 2.5.1. Let w(t) be a function satisfying the conditions in the hypoth-

2.7       Initial shifting of contours

To continue with our evaluation of Q(α, β, γ, δ), the idea is to move the lines of in-
tegration in (2.6.7) and write Q(α, β, γ, δ) in terms of the residues of F(s1 , s2 , s3 , s4 )
at its poles. This turns out to be a rather involved process, and will occupy us
for the next few sections. Recall that we use the notation sj = σj + itj , that
L = log T , and that A denotes a positive absolute constant that is not necessarily
the same for each instance. Recall also that we are assuming (2.6.2). The more
trivial estimations will be left to the reader.

   We first truncate the lines of integration in (2.6.7), as follows. For m =
1, 2, 3, 4, let Em be the line segment from ηm −imL17 to ηm +imL17 . The exponent
17 in L17 is arbitrary, and the discussions that follow remain valid if we replace 17
by any larger real number. Since ηm = cm /L and y = T θ , it follows that y sj  1
when σj = ηj . Using this, Lemma 2.2.2, and (2.6.6), we can deduce from (2.6.7)
and (2.6.8) that
               Z Z Z Z
          1
 Q =                       F(s1 , s2 , s3 , s4 ) ds4 ds3 ds2 ds1 + O(L−A ). (2.7.1)
        (2πi)4 E1 E2 E3 E4
   The next step is to move E4 to the left and write Q in terms of an integral
of residues of F. Let c > 0 be an absolute constant that is so small that we
                                                                                                   63



can apply Lemma 2.2.2 to the zeta-functions in the definition (2.6.8) of F when
−c(log L)−1 ≤ σ4 ≤ 1 and sj ∈ Ej for j = 1, 2, 3. We can take c = c0 /99, say,
where c0 is as in Lemma 2.2.2. Let D4 be the line segment from −c(log L)−1 −i4L17
to −c(log L)−1 + i4L17 . We move E4 to D4 and proceed in a way similar to that
in the above discussion containing equations (2.4.13) through (2.4.16). That is,
we integrate F with respect to s4 along the rectangle determined by E4 and D4 .
We use the residue theorem to write that the integral equals a sum of residues of
F. Then we integrate both sides of the resulting equation with respect to s3 , s2 ,
and s1 along E3 , E2 , and E1 , respectively. The contribution of the integrals along
the horizontal sides of the rectangle is small because of Lemma 2.2.2, (2.6.6), the
bound y s1 +s2 +s3 +s4  1, and the small size of the factor s−3
                                                              4 of F. Similarly, the

contribution of the integral along D4 is small because y s4  y −c/ log L for s4 ∈ D4 .
The result we arrive at from (2.7.1) and this process of moving E4 to D4 is

    We treat each of the nine integrals         R`ν in (2.7.3) one by one in the following
sections. As mentioned at the beginning of this section, the basic idea is to
move the paths of integration in (2.7.3) and express Q as a sum of residues of
F. When we “move” one path of integration to another, we always do so by
connecting the two paths with horizontal line segments and then applying the
residue theorem to write one path integral in terms of the other. The contributions
of the integrals along the horizontal line segments are always bounded by  L−A .
These bounds follow from using y sj  1, Lemma 2.2.2, (2.6.6), and the fact that
|sm | ≥ |tm |  L17 for some m = 1, 2, 3, 4. The calculations involved in bounding
the integrals of these horizontal segments are straightforward, and are thus left
to the reader. From now on, we will move paths of integration without further
mention of the horizontal line segments.

    Another thing to keep in mind in the calculations below is that the factors
ζ −1 (1 ± sj + ξ) with ξ = α, β, γ, or δ are holomorphic functions of sj in the
domains we are concerned with. This is because we will only work within the
known zero-free region of ζ(s) given by Lemma 2.2.2. Also, the Euler product F
defined in (2.6.5) is holomorphic by (2.6.6). Thus we can determine the poles of
each R`ν by looking only at the factors s−1
                                         j  and ζ(1 + sj ± sk ) in our expression
for R`ν .

                     U1 + · · · + Ur ≤ T < U1 + · · · + Ur + Ur+1 .

  U1 + · · · + Ur > T − Ur+1 = T − (T + U1 + U2 + · · · + Ur )b ≥ T − (2T )b .
                                                                                       91



Hence, since T − (2T )b = (1 + o(1))T , it follows that

                          (1 + o(1))T < U1 + · · · + Ur ≤ T.                     (2.18.7)

By (2.18.6), we can write

for j = 1, 2, 3, . . . , r. Add the corresponding sides of these r inequalities to deduce
that
       π               U + ··· + U
                          1         r

                  π             T

                      π                     T

2.19         Numerical computations

We can use any standard computer package to compute the coefficients of the
polynomial R(t; y, ρ) in (2.15.2). By such numerical computations we are able to
determine the values of the coefficients λm,n in (2.15.5) and see that
                         724 4 8      14684 2 6       2896 3 6    64 4

                          2 −4   64 −3   10 −2  68
              C(0, θ) =     θ  +    θ  +   θ   + θ−1 + 1.
                          9      45      3      21

This agrees with the conjecture (6.29) of Conrey and Snaith [19].

   With the above explicit form of C(a, θ), we can numerically optimize the value
of the lower bound (2.18.8) for κ. For fixed θ close to 1/48, we find that the value
a ≈ 4.3120526 is optimal, and we arrive at the lower bound

                                  κ > 0.0001049.

If we assume the (yet unproved) statement that Theorems 1.1.2 and 1.1.3 hold for
any θ > 0, then we can optimize numerically by varying both a and θ. We find
that the values a ≈ 3.0407338 and θ ≈ 0.9967280 are optimal, and they give

                                  κ > 0.0086729.

3       Zeros of the Riemann
        zeta-function near the
        critical line

In this chapter, we prove the results described in Section 1.2. As in the previous
chapter, we follow common practice in analytic number theory and let ε denote
an arbitrarily small positive constant that is not necessarily the same in each
instance. We also use the standard notation e(z) for exp(2πiz). We consider the
variables T > 1 and y > 1 as large parameters going to infinity.



3.1     Lemmas

We first state and prove a number of technical lemmas we will need in our proofs.
The following lemma contains some basic estimates for the function W (ξ) defined
by (1.2.7). Its proof is reminiscent of that of Lemma 2.1 of Soundararajan [61].

3.2      Relating the density of zeros to
         the second moment

In this section, we relate N (σ, T ) to the mollified second moment (1.2.4) via
standard arguments using Littlewood’s lemma. Here and in the rest of the chapter,

we assume that 21 + log1 y ≤ σ ≤ 21 + 0 , with 0 > 0 small enough and y > 1 large
enough that the conditions in Lemma 3.1.1 are satisfied. First, observe that (ii)

equals 1. We choose b0 > 1 such that the error term in (3.2.1) has modulus ≤ 12
for Re(s) ≥ b0 . Our choice implies that ζ(s)M (s) has no zeros with real part ≥ b0 .

      Let ν(ξ; T, 2T ) denote the number of zeros β1 + iγ1 of ζ(s)M (s) that have
β1 > ξ and T < γ1 ≤ 2T . Since a zero of ζ(s) is also a zero of ζ(s)M (s), it follows

        Z 2T
             |ζ(σ + it)M (σ + it)|2 dt ≥ T + O(∆ log T ) + O(T y 1−2σ ).
            T

From this, (3.6.10), (3.6.11), and the definition y = T θ , we arrive at
                Z 2T
                     |ζ(σ + it)M (σ + it)|2 dt = T + O(T y 1−2σ ).
                       T

This completes the proof of Theorem 1.2.2.

4       The pair correlation
        conjecture and the
        alternative hypothesis

In this chapter, we prove the results described in Section 1.3. As usual, we consider
the variable T > 1 as a large parameter going to infinity.



4.1      Lemmas

In each of the following lemmas we assume there is a number T0 such that the func-
tion f (α, T ) is defined for α ≥ 0 and T ≥ T0 , and f (·, T ) is Lebesgue measurable
for each such T .

4.3       Proof of Theorem 1.3.3

Recall that g(α) = |α| for |α| ≤ 1, and is defined for all R by periodicity. Let ν be
the measure on [0, ∞) defined by ν(0) = 12 , ν(2n) = 1 for all integers n ≥ 1, and
dν(α) = g(α) dα for 2n < α < 2n + 2, n ≥ 0. When µ = ν, we can easily evaluate
the expressions in (A), (B), and (C) involving µ and see that the statements (A),
(B), and (C) are the same as (A2), (B2), and (C2), respectively. Thus (A2), (B2),
and (C2) are equivalent statements by Theorem 1.3.2. However, showing that
they are equivalent to (D2) is not as straightforward. To do this, we will define a
new statement (D0 ) and show that, when µ = ν, the statement (D) is equivalent
to (D0 ), which in turn is equivalent to (D2).

Remark 1 : As we mentioned in Section 1.3, Theorem 1.3.2 may be used to prove
Theorem 1.3.1 by taking µ to be the measure defined by µ(0) = 1/2, dµ(α) = αdα
for 0 < α ≤ 1, and dµ(α) = dα for α > 1. The process in doing this is similar to
the one in this section.



Remark 2 : A stronger conclusion holds in Lemma 4.1.2 in the special case when
r(α) = (sin βα/α)2 and r1 (α) = α−2 . Namely, the conclusion (4.1.17) of the
                                                                                170



lemma holds uniformly for all β in any fixed closed interval. It follows from this
and our proof of Theorem 1.3.2 that if (A) holds, then (D) holds uniformly for
all β in any fixed, closed interval. Therefore, by our proof of Lemma 4.3.1, if (A)
holds, then (D0 ) holds uniformly for all β in any fixed, closed subinterval of an
open interval on which ϕ0 (β) exists and is continuous. Hence, by our proof of
Theorem 1.3.3, if (D2) holds, then it holds uniformly for all β in any fixed closed
subinterval of [0, ∞) not containing half-integers. Similarly, if (D1) holds, then
it holds uniformly on any fixed closed interval. This uniformity in (D1) was first
observed by Gallagher and Mueller [29].

             #{0 < γn ≤ T : (γn+1 − γn )ψ(γn ) ≥ δ} = o(T log T ).

Proof. Recall the properties of the function ψ(γ) from the statement of the Al-
ternative Hypothesis. Define

                                Ψ0 (T ) = max ψ(γn ).                             (4.4.17)

as T → ∞. It follows from (4.4.17) that

#{0 < γn ≤ T : (γn+1 −γn )ψ(γn ) ≥ δ} ≤ #{0 < γn ≤ T : γn+1 −γn ≥ λ/ log T },

where λ = δ log T /Ψ0 (T ). By a result of Fujii (see §9.25 of [64]), we have

   #{0 < γn ≤ T : γn+1 − γn ≥ λ/ log T }  N (T ) exp(−Aλ1/2 (log λ)−1/4 ).
                                                                                (4.4.19)
The Lemma now follows from (4.4.18), (4.4.19), (4.3.13), and the fact that λ → ∞
as T → ∞.

   Next we prove the lower bound for p1/2 . To do this, we first consider the set

This implies (1.3.9) and completes the proof of Theorem 1.3.4.



Remark 3 : The upper bounds in (1.3.6), (1.3.7), and (4.4.26) can be proved using
the assumption that N ∗ (T ) ∼ 1 instead of the stronger assumption that all the
zeros are simple. To do this, we only need to define the sets Ek/2 (T ) so that
they count multiplicity. However, we did use the hypothesis that all the zeros are
simple to prove the lower bound for p1/2 . This is because we required Ek/2 (T ) to
count multiplicity to be able to use (D2), and at the same time we needed Ek/2 (T )
to not count multiplicity in proving (4.4.28).
